Each state is allocated two electors plus one additional elector for every congressional district in the state, which in effect combines to equal the number of elected officials that state sends to Congress. The District of Columbia, with no voting representation in Congress, is allocated three electoral votes.[^cite_note-285] Electoral College votes are officially cast by individual electors selected under procedures established by [state law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_legislature_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-Review-Electors-286][^cite_note-287] In practice, 48 states and the District of Columbia require their electors to vote for the statewide popular-vote winner; Maine and Nebraska award two votes to the statewide winner and one to the winner in each congressional district.[^cite_note-Review-ElectoralAllocation-288] Both the president and the vice president serve a four-year term, and the president may be [reelected to the office only once](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-second_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution), for one additional four-year term.&#x20;

#### Judiciary

The [U.S. federal judiciary](1636627.md) consists primarily of the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md), the [U.S. courts of appeals](70228.md), and the [U.S. district courts](70235.md). Judges for these courts are appointed under [Article III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Three_of_the_United_States_Constitution): They are nominated by the president, confirmed by the Senate, and hold office subject to good behavior (generally resulting in life tenure). To limit outside interference, their compensation may not be reduced while they remain in office.[^cite_note-Review-FederalJudges-289] Congress has also created [Article I courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_I_tribunal) under its legislative powers; unlike Article III judges, whose tenure and compensation are protected by the Constitution, Article I judges generally serve fixed statutory terms.[^cite_note-290]&#x20;

Federal district courts are the primary trial courts of the federal judiciary and hear civil and criminal cases within their jurisdiction, including cases arising under federal statutes, the Constitution, or [international treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_treaties).[^cite_note-Review-FederalCourts-291] Decisions of federal district courts may generally be challenged to a federal court of appeals. The U.S. judicial system's 12 regional circuits divide the country into geographic regions for appellate purposes, while the [Federal Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Federal_Circuit) has nationwide jurisdiction over specified types of cases.[^cite_note-Review-FederalCourts-291]&#x20;

The Supreme Court is the highest court in the federal judiciary.[^cite_note-FedJud-292] It has both original and appellate jurisdiction and exercises [judicial review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_review_in_the_United_States), under which it may hold laws or governmental actions unconstitutional.[^cite_note-FedJud-292] On average, the Supreme Court receives about 7,000 petitions for [writs of certiorari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writ_of_certiorari) each year but grants review in about 80 cases.[^cite_note-293]&#x20;

The Supreme Court consists of nine justices, including the [chief justice of the United States](31739.md), and decides cases before it by majority vote. When the Senate conducts an impeachment trial of a sitting president, the chief justice presides. Like other Article III judges, Supreme Court justices are nominated by the president and confirmed by the Senate and hold office during good behavior.[^cite_note-Review-FederalJudges-289]&#x20;

### Subdivisions

Main articles: [U.S. state](18618239.md), [County (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_\(United_States\)), [Indian country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_country), [Territories of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States), and [Local government in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_the_United_States)

Further information: [List of states and territories of the United States](12610470.md), [List of federally recognized tribes by state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federally_recognized_tribes_by_state), and [Federally recognized tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federally_recognized_tribe)

Territories of the United States

[Territories of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States) include [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa), [Guam](11974.md), the [Northern Mariana Islands](21402.md), [Puerto Rico](23041.md), and the [U.S. Virgin Islands](32135.md).

In the [U.S. federal system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism_in_the_United_States), sovereign powers are shared between three levels of government specified in the Constitution: the federal government, the states, and Indian tribes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1997-294][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen1942-295] The U.S. also asserts sovereignty over five permanently inhabited territories: [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa), [Guam](11974.md), the [Northern Mariana Islands](21402.md), [Puerto Rico](23041.md), and the [U.S. Virgin Islands](32135.md).[^cite_note-HRI-2012-33]&#x20;

Residents of the 50 states are governed by their elected state government, under [state constitutions compatible with the national constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_constitutions_in_the_United_States), and by elected [local governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_the_United_States) that are administrative divisions of a state.[^cite_note-296] States are subdivided into counties or county equivalents, which (except in Hawaii) can permit the formation of municipalities administered by their own elected representatives. The District of Columbia is [a federal district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_district_of_the_United_States) containing the U.S. capital, [Washington, D.C.](108956.md)[^cite_note-297] The federal district is an administrative division of the federal government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldstein2017-298]&#x20;



Map of the [326 Indian reservations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_reservations_in_the_United_States) in the United States; 231 recognized [Alaska Native tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Alaska_Native_tribal_entities) are not shown.

As of January 2026, there were 575 [federally recognized tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federally_recognized_tribe).[^cite_note-Review-Tribes2026-299] There are approximately [326 Indian reservations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_reservations_in_the_United_States). The tribes hold a government-to-government relationship with the U.S. federal government in Washington and are legally defined as [domestic dependent nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_dependent_nations) with [inherent tribal sovereignty rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribal_sovereignty_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen1942-295][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1997-294][^cite_note-300]\[^cite_note-[www.justice.gov-2017-301](http://www.justice.gov-2017-301/)]&#x20;

In addition to the five major territories, the U.S. also asserts sovereignty over the [United States Minor Outlying Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Minor_Outlying_Islands) in the [Pacific Ocean](23070.md) and the [Caribbean](18956035.md).[^cite_note-HRI-2012-33] The seven undisputed islands without permanent populations are [Baker Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baker_Island), [Howland Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howland_Island), [Jarvis Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarvis_Island), [Johnston Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnston_Atoll), [Kingman Reef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingman_Reef), [Midway Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midway_Atoll), and [Palmyra Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_Atoll). U.S. sovereignty over the unpopulated [Bajo Nuevo Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajo_Nuevo_Bank), [Navassa Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navassa_Island), [Serranilla Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serranilla_Bank), and [Wake Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wake_Island) is disputed.[^cite_note-HRI-2012-33]&#x20;



### Political parties

Main article: [Political parties in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_parties_in_the_United_States)

Further information: [List of political parties in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_the_United_States) and [Political party strength in U.S. states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_U.S._states)



States and territories by partisan control, as of February 2025:&#x20;

&#x20; [Democratic Party](5043544.md)

&#x20; [Republican Party](32070.md)

&#x20; [New Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Progressive_Party_\(Puerto_Rico\))

&#x20; Divided partisan control

The Constitution is silent on political parties. However, they developed independently in the 18th century with the [Federalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_Party) and [Anti-Federalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Federalist_Party) parties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHofstadter1969iv-302] Since then, the United States has operated as a *de facto* [two-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party_system), though the parties have changed over time.[^cite_note-Blake-2021-303]&#x20;

Since the mid-19th century, the two main national parties have been the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) and the [Republican Party](32070.md). The Democratic Party is generally described as sitting on the [center](39619035.md) to [center-left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-left_politics) of the [political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_spectrum), with [a platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\)) rooted in [modern American liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-\:7-304] while the Republican Party is generally described as [right-wing](56522.md), with [a platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_the_Republican_Party_\(United_States\)) rooted in [American conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-\:7-304] Since the 2010s, a number of political scientists have characterized the Republican Party as having shifted toward [right-wing populism](8354382.md) and [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism), with some [comparative political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparative_politics) studies classifying it as a [far-right](221220.md) party.[^cite_note-\:4-264][^cite_note-\:7-304] Surveys have also found that Democrats have become more consistently liberal and Republicans more consistently conservative, reducing ideological overlap between the two parties.[^cite_note-305]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main articles: [Foreign relations of the United States](24296062.md) and [Foreign policy of the United States](7564.md)

The United Nations headquarters

The [United Nations headquarters](188955.md) has been situated along the [East River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_River) in [Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan) since 1952; in 1945, the United States was a [founding member of the UN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_member_of_the_United_Nations).

The United States has an established structure of foreign relations, with the world's [second-largest diplomatic corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_diplomatic_missions) as of 2024. It is a [permanent member of the United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council)[^cite_note-306] and home to the [United Nations headquarters](188955.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''United_Nations_Headquarters_Agreement''1948-307] The United States is a member of the [G7](1196634.md),[^cite_note-308] [G20](2206608.md),[^cite_note-309] and [OECD](33853117.md) intergovernmental organizations.[^cite_note-310] [Almost all countries have embassies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_in_the_United_States) and many have [consulates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consul_\(representative\)) (official representatives) in the country. Likewise, nearly all countries host formal [diplomatic missions](8970.md) with the United States, except [Iran](310477.md),[^cite_note-311] [North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korea%E2%80%93United_States_relations),[^cite_note-312] and [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Bhutan#Other_countries).[^cite_note-313] Though [Taiwan](15996902.md) does not have formal diplomatic relations with the U.S., it maintains close unofficial relations.[^cite_note-314] The United States regularly [supplies Taiwan with military equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six_Assurances) to deter potential Chinese aggression.[^cite_note-315] The country's geopolitical attention has increasingly turned to the [Indo-Pacific](330456.md), where the U.S. joined the [Quadrilateral Security Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrilateral_Security_Dialogue) and [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadan2022-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]&#x20;

The United States has a "[Special Relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Relationship)" [with the United Kingdom](1814362.md)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDumbrellSchäfer200945-319] and strong ties [with Canada](5199.md),[^cite_note-320] [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93United_States_relations),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVaughn2008-321] [New Zealand](12792048.md),[^cite_note-322] [the Philippines](15994099.md),[^cite_note-323] [Japan](2991012.md),[^cite_note-324] [South Korea](5748222.md),[^cite_note-325] [Israel](3449549.md),[^cite_note-326] and several [European Union countries](10890716.md) such as [France](686250.md), [Italy](15988700.md), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany%E2%80%93United_States_relations), [Spain](15420327.md), and [Poland](10858944.md).[^cite_note-327] The U.S. works closely with its [NATO](21133.md) allies on military and [national security](240468.md) issues, and with countries in the Americas through the [Organization of American States](22788.md) and the [United States–Mexico–Canada Free Trade Agreement](58620732.md). The U.S. exercises full international defense authority and responsibility for [Micronesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_States_of_Micronesia), the [Marshall Islands](19147.md), and [Palau](23372.md) through the [Compact of Free Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compact_of_Free_Association).[^cite_note-Review-COFA-328] It has increasingly conducted strategic cooperation [with India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_relations),[^cite_note-329] while [its ties with China](277880.md) have steadily deteriorated.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331] Beginning in 2014, the U.S. had become [a key ally of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93United_States_relations).[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333]&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [United States Armed Forces](32212.md)

The Pentagon

[The Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon), the headquarters of the [U.S. Department of Defense](7279897.md) in [Arlington County, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington_County,_Virginia), is one of the world's largest office buildings with over 6.5 million square feet (600,000 m

2

) of [floor space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floor_space).

The president is the [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-Chief_of_the_United_States) of the United States Armed Forces and appoints its leaders, the [secretary of defense](43998.md) and the [Joint Chiefs of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Chiefs_of_Staff). The [Department of Defense](7279897.md), headquartered at [the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) near Washington, D.C., administers five of the six service branches, which are made up of the [U.S. Army](32087.md), [Marine Corps](17349325.md), [Navy](20518076.md), [Air Force](32090.md), and [Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force).[^cite_note-334] The [Coast Guard](32223.md) is administered by the [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) in peacetime and can be transferred to the [Department of the Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_the_Navy) in wartime.[^cite_note-335] Total strength of the entire military is about 1.3 million active duty with an additional 400,000 in reserve.&#x20;

The United States military is widely regarded as the most powerful and advanced in the world.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337] The U.S. [spent \$954 billion on its military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_budget_of_the_United_States) in 2025, which is by far the [largest amount of any country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_with_highest_military_expenditures), making up 33% of global military spending and accounting for 3.1% of the country's GDP.[^cite_note-SIPRI-2020-338] The [U.S. possesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_of_the_United_States) 42% of the world's nuclear weapons—the second-largest stockpile after [that of Russia](588941.md).[^cite_note-Stockholm_International_Peace_Research_Institute-2024-339] The United States has the [third-largest combined armed forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_military_and_paramilitary_personnel) in the world, behind the [Chinese People's Liberation Army](66890.md) and [Indian Armed Forces](7171338.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHackett2023-340] In addition to the vast network of military bases on its soil, the U.S. maintains approximately 800 other bases and installations around the world,[^cite_note-341] and it [deploys greater than 100 active-duty personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_military_deployments) in each of 25 foreign countries.[^cite_note-342] The United States has engaged in over 400 military interventions since its founding in 1776, with over half of these occurring between 1950 and 2019 and 25% occurring in the post–Cold War era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKushiToft2023-343]&#x20;

[State defense forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_defense_forces) (SDFs) are military units that operate under the sole authority of a state government. SDFs are authorized by state and federal law but are under the command of [the state's governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346] By contrast, the 54 [U.S. National Guard](37988.md) organizations[^cite_note-347] fall under the dual control of [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_governments_of_the_United_States) or [territorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States) governments and the [federal government](195149.md). The National Guard personnel of a state or territory can be federalized by the president under the [National Defense Act Amendments of 1933](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Act_of_1916#National_Defense_Act_Amendments_of_1933); this legislation created the Guard and provides for the integration of [Army National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_National_Guard) and [Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_National_Guard) units and personnel into the U.S. Army and (since 1947) the U.S. Air Force.[^cite_note-348] SDFs cannot be federalized.[^cite_note-349] The total number of National Guard members is about 430,000, while the estimated combined strength of SDFs is less than 10,000.[^cite_note-350]&#x20;

### Law enforcement and criminal justice

Main articles: [Law of the United States](27552742.md), [Law enforcement in the United States](4876283.md), and [Crime in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_the_United_States)



[J. Edgar Hoover Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Edgar_Hoover_Building), the headquarters of the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI), in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md)

There are about 18,000 U.S. police agencies from local to national level in the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanksHendrixHickman20161-351] Law in the United States is mainly enforced by local police departments and [sheriff departments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheriffs_in_the_United_States) in their municipal or county jurisdictions. [The state police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_police_\(United_States\)) departments [have authority in their respective state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_power_\(United_States_constitutional_law\)), and [federal agencies](16252377.md) such as the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI) and the [U.S. Marshals Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Marshals_Service) have national jurisdiction and specialized duties, such as protecting [civil rights](37071.md), [national security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_security_of_the_United_States), enforcing [U.S. federal courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._federal_courts)' rulings and federal laws, and interstate criminal activity.[^cite_note-352] [State courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_court_\(United_States\)) conduct almost all civil and criminal trials,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEManweller200655-353] while federal courts [adjudicate the much smaller number of civil and criminal cases that relate to federal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subject-matter_jurisdiction).[^cite_note-354]&#x20;

There is no unified "criminal justice system" in the United States. The [American prison system](1021698.md) is largely heterogenous, with thousands of relatively independent systems operating across federal, state, local, and tribal levels. In 2026, "these systems hold [nearly 2 million people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_incarceration_rate) in 1,566 state prisons, 98 federal prisons, 3,116 local jails, 1,277 juvenile correctional facilities, 220 immigration detention facilities, and 77 Indian country jails, as well as in military prisons, civil commitment centers, state psychiatric hospitals, and prisons in the U.S. territories—at a system-wide cost of at least \$445 billion each year."[^cite_note-Sawyer-2026-355]&#x20;

Despite disparate systems of confinement, four main institutions dominate: [federal prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_federal_prisons), [state prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_United_States_state_prisons), local jails, and [juvenile correctional facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_juvenile_justice_system).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETravisWesternRedburn2014-356] Federal prisons are run by the [Federal Bureau of Prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Bureau_of_Prisons) and hold pretrial detainees as well as people who have been convicted of federal crimes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETravisWesternRedburn2014-356] State prisons, run by the [department of corrections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrections) of each state, hold people sentenced and serving prison time (usually longer than one year) for felony offenses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETravisWesternRedburn2014-356] Local jails are county or municipal facilities that incarcerate defendants prior to trial; they also hold those serving short sentences (typically under a year).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETravisWesternRedburn2014-356] Juvenile correctional facilities are operated by local or state governments and serve as longer-term placements for any [minor adjudicated as delinquent](348729.md) and ordered by a judge to be confined.[^cite_note-357]&#x20;

In January 2023, the United States had the [sixth-highest per capita incarceration rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_incarceration_rate) in the world—531 people per 100,000 inhabitants—and the largest prison and jail population in the world, with more than 1.9 million people incarcerated.[^cite_note-Sawyer-2025-358][^cite_note-359][^cite_note-WorldPrisonBrief-360] An analysis of the [World Health Organization](33583.md) Mortality Database from 2010 showed U.S. homicide rates "were 7 times higher than in other high-income countries, driven by [a gun homicide rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_deaths_in_the_United_States) that was 25 times higher".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrinshteynHemenway2016-361]&#x20;

The country's legal system is classified by some political scientists and legal scholars as operating under a "partial [rule of law](25166191.md)" system, according to two 2026 surveys conducted by [Bright Line Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bright_Line_Watch) and the [London School of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Economics).[^cite_note-\:5-271]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of the United States](32022.md)

See also: [Lists of companies of the United States by state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_companies_of_the_United_States_by_state)

Obverse of a US\$1 bill

The [U.S. dollar](18717338.md) is the most-used currency [in international transactions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_use_of_the_U.S._dollar) and the world's foremost [reserve currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_currency).

The U.S. has a highly developed [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy)[^cite_note-362] that has been the world's largest nominally [since about 1890](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_largest_historical_GDP).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFordham2017-363] Its 2024 [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP)[^cite_note-PPP-26] of more than \$29 trillion[^cite_note-364] constituted over 25% of nominal [global economic output](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_world_product), or 15% at [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) (PPP). The country ranks [first in the world by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)),[^cite_note-365] [second when adjusted for purchasing power parities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) (PPP),[^cite_note-IMFWEO.US-25] and [ninth by PPP-adjusted GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita).[^cite_note-IMFWEO.US-25] In August 2026, the total [U.S. federal government debt](322221.md) passed \$40 trillion, having more than doubled in the previous decade.[^cite_note-366]&#x20;



[New York City](645042.md) is the world's principal [financial center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_center), and [its metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area) is the world's [largest metropolitan economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP).

Of the world's [500 largest companies by revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_Global_500), [138 were headquartered in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_companies_in_the_United_States_by_revenue) in 2025,[^cite_note-Fortune-2025-367] the highest number of any country.[^cite_note-368] The [U.S. dollar](18717338.md) is the currency most used [in international transactions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_use_of_the_U.S._dollar) and the world's foremost [reserve currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_currency), backed by the country's dominant economy, [its military](32212.md), the [petrodollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrodollar) system, its large [U.S. treasuries market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Treasury), and its linked [eurodollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurodollar).[^cite_note-federalreserve.gov-369] [Several countries use it as their official currency](18717338.md#Countries_that_use_US_dollar), and in others it is the [*de facto* currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_facto_currency).[^cite_note-Benjamin_J._Cohen_2006,_p._17-370][^cite_note-371] The U.S. has [free trade agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements) with [several countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements_of_the_United_States), including the [USMCA](58620732.md).[^cite_note-372] Although the United States has reached a [post-industrial level of economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-industrial_economy)[^cite_note-Collins-2023-373] and is often described as having a [service economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_economy),[^cite_note-Collins-2023-373][^cite_note-Econ-374] it remains a major industrial power;[^cite_note-375] in 2024, the [U.S. manufacturing sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._manufacturing_sector) was the world's [second-largest by value output](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_manufacturing_output) after [China's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing_in_China).[^cite_note-376]&#x20;



The [New York Stock Exchange](21560.md) on [Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street), the world's [largest stock exchange by market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges#Major_stock_exchanges)

[^cite_note-NYSEhighestcap-377]

[New York City](645042.md) is the world's principal [financial center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_center),[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-NYCFintechAndFinancialCapitalWorld-379] and [its metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area) is the world's [largest metropolitan economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP).[^cite_note-NYCEpicenterUSMetroEconomy-380] The [New York Stock Exchange](21560.md) and [Nasdaq](21559.md), both located in New York City, are the world's two [largest stock exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges) by [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization) and [trade volume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_volume).[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-sfc.hk-382] The United States is at the forefront of [technological advancement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_the_United_States) and [innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation) in many economic fields, especially in [artificial intelligence](1164.md); [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics) and [computers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer); [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceuticals); and medical, [aerospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerospace) and [military equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_equipment).[^cite_note-CIA-2018-383] The country's economy is fueled by abundant [natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resource), a well-developed [infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure_policy_of_the_United_States), and [high productivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_labour_productivity).[^cite_note-Wright,_Gavin_2007_p._185-384] The [largest trading partners of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_the_United_States) are the [European Union](9317.md), Mexico, Canada, China, Japan, South Korea, the United Kingdom, Vietnam, India, and Taiwan.[^cite_note-385] The United States is the world's [largest importer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_imports) and [second-largest exporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports).[^cite_note-388] It is by far the world's [largest exporter of services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_service_exports).[^cite_note-389]&#x20;

Americans have the highest average [household](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_income)[^cite_note-390] and [employee income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_average_wage) among OECD member states, and the fourth-highest [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) in 2023,[^cite_note-391] up from sixth-highest in 2013.[^cite_note-Household_Income-392] With personal [consumption expenditures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_spending) of over \$18.5 trillion in 2023,[^cite_note-393] the U.S. has a heavily [consumer-driven economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_economy) and is the world's [largest consumer market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_consumer_markets).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESteenkamp2024-394] The U.S. [ranked first in the number of dollar billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_billionaires) and [millionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_millionaires) in 2023, with 735 billionaires and nearly 22 million millionaires.[^cite_note-395]&#x20;

[Wealth in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_in_the_United_States) is highly concentrated; in 2011, the richest 10% of the adult population owned 72% of the country's household wealth, while the bottom 50% owned just 2%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiketty2014\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsisbn_9780674430006page257_257\]-396] [U.S. wealth inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality_in_the_United_States) increased substantially since the late 1980s,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmithZidarZwick2022-397] and [income inequality in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_the_United_States) reached a record high in 2019.[^cite_note-398] In 2024, the country had some of the highest wealth and income inequality levels among [OECD](33853117.md) countries.[^cite_note-399] Since the 1970s, there has been a decoupling of U.S. wage gains from worker productivity,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHopkin2020-400] while the economy has become more dominated by [financial services and stock trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_capitalism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGottschalk202515-401] In 2016, the top fifth of earners took home more than half of all income,[^cite_note-402] giving the U.S. one of the widest income distributions among OECD countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmeeding2005-403][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHopkin2020-400] There were about 771,480 [homeless persons in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness_in_the_United_States) on a single night in January 2024.[^cite_note-Review-HUD2024-404][^cite_note-405] In 2022, 6.4 million children experienced food insecurity.[^cite_note-ers.usda.gov-406] [Feeding America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feeding_America) estimates that around one in five, or approximately 13 million, [children experience hunger in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunger_in_the_United_States#Children) and do not know where or when they will get their next meal.[^cite_note-FactsAbout-407] Also in 2022, about 37.9 million people, or 11.5% of the U.S. population, were [living in poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-408]&#x20;

The United States has a smaller [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) and redistributes less income through government action than most other [high-income countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank_high-income_economy).[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERank2023116–117-410] It is the only [advanced economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_economy) that does not [guarantee its workers paid vacation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_statutory_minimum_employment_leave_by_country) nationally[^cite_note-411] and one of a few countries in the world without [paid family leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parental_leave_in_the_United_States) as a legal right.[^cite_note-412] The United States has a higher percentage of low-income [workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_class_in_the_United_States) than almost any other developed country, largely because of a weak [collective bargaining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_bargaining) system and lack of government support for at-risk workers.[^cite_note-413]&#x20;

### Science and technology

Main articles: [Science and technology in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_the_United_States) and [Science policy of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_policy_of_the_United_States)



California's [Silicon Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Valley) is the largest and foremost technology and innovation hub in the world.

[^cite_note-414]

[^cite_note-415]

The United States [has been a leader in technological innovation since the late 19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_and_industrial_history_of_the_United_States) and scientific research since the mid-20th century.[^cite_note-416] Methods for producing [interchangeable parts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interchangeable_parts) and the establishment of a [machine tool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_tool) industry enabled [the large-scale manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_system_of_manufacturing) of U.S. consumer products in the late 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoodfriendMcDermott2021-417] By the early 20th century, factory [electrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrification), the introduction of the [assembly line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_line), and other [labor-saving techniques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automation) created the system of [mass production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_production).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHounshell1984-418]&#x20;

In the 21st century, the United States continues to be one of the world's foremost scientific powers,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETollefsonVan_Noorden2024-419] though China has emerged as a major competitor in many fields.[^cite_note-420] The U.S. has the [highest research and development expenditures of any country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_by_research_and_development_spending)[^cite_note-421] and ranks ninth as a percentage of GDP.[^cite_note-422] In 2022, the United States was (after China) the country with the [second-highest number of published scientific papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_scientific_and_technical_journal_articles).[^cite_note-423] In 2021, the U.S. ranked second (also after China) by the number of patent applications, and third by trademark and industrial design applications (after China and Germany), according to [World Intellectual Property Indicators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Intellectual_Property_Indicators).[^cite_note-424] In 2025[^cite_note-425][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuttaLanvin202519-426] the United States ranked third (after Switzerland and Sweden) in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index). The United States is considered to be a world leader in the development of [artificial intelligence](1164.md) technology.[^cite_note-427] In 2023, the United States was ranked the second most technologically advanced country in the world (after South Korea) by *[Global Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Finance_\(magazine\))* magazine.[^cite_note-428]&#x20;

#### Spaceflight

Main article: [Space policy of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_policy_of_the_United_States)



Astronaut [Buzz Aldrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzz_Aldrin) (with [Neil Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Armstrong) seen in visor reflection) during the 1969 [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) mission, the [first of six](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) crewed [Moon landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landings). The United States is the only country to have landed humans on the Moon.

The United States has maintained a space program since the late 1950s, beginning with the establishment of the [National Aeronautics and Space Administration](18426568.md) (NASA) in 1958.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] In 1961, the United States became the second country (after the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union)) to successfully [launch a human into space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_spaceflight). NASA's [Apollo program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) (1961–1972) achieved the first crewed [Moon landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing) with the 1969 [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) mission; it remains one of the agency's most significant milestones.[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432] Other major endeavors by NASA include the [Space Shuttle program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Shuttle_program) (1981–2011),[^cite_note-433] the [Voyager program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voyager_program) (1972–present), the [Hubble](40203.md) and [James Webb](434221.md) [space telescopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_telescope) (launched in 1990 and 2021, respectively),[^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435] and the multi-mission [Mars Exploration Program](32506368.md) (*[Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirit_\(rover\))*, *[Opportunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opportunity_\(rover\))*, *[Curiosity](36645032.md)*, and *[Perseverance](63297153.md)*).[^cite_note-436] NASA is one of five agencies collaborating on the [International Space Station](15043.md) (ISS);[^cite_note-437] U.S. contributions to the ISS include several modules, including *[Destiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destiny_\(ISS_module\))* (2001), *[Harmony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmony_\(ISS_module\))* (2007), and *[Tranquility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tranquility_\(ISS_module\))* (2010), as well as ongoing logistical and operational support.[^cite_note-438]&#x20;

The United States [private sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector) dominates the global [commercial spaceflight industry](752732.md).[^cite_note-439] Prominent American spaceflight contractors include [Blue Origin](887418.md), [Boeing](18933266.md), [Lockheed Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin), [Northrop Grumman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman), and [SpaceX](832774.md). NASA programs such as the [Commercial Crew Program](64072812.md), [Commercial Resupply Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Resupply_Services), [Commercial Lunar Payload Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_Lunar_Payload_Services), and [NextSTEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_Space_Technologies_for_Exploration_Partnerships) have facilitated growing private-sector involvement in American spaceflight.[^cite_note-440]&#x20;

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in the United States](7635170.md)

In 2023, the United States received approximately 84% of its energy from fossil fuel, and its largest source of energy was [petroleum](19086358.md) (38%), followed by [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_in_the_United_States) (36%), [renewable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_the_United_States) (9%), [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_in_the_United_States) (9%), and [nuclear power](4131402.md) (9%).[^cite_note-441][^cite_note-visu-442] In 2022, the United States constituted about 4% of the [world's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population), but [consumed around 16%](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_energy_consumption) of the [world's energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_energy_consumption).[^cite_note-443] The U.S. ranks as the [second-highest emitter of greenhouse gases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_greenhouse_gas_emissions) behind China.[^cite_note-444]&#x20;

The U.S. is the world's [largest producer of nuclear power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_by_country), generating around 30% of the world's nuclear electricity.[^cite_note-445] It also has the highest number of nuclear power reactors of any country.[^cite_note-446] From 2024, the U.S. plans to triple its nuclear power capacity by 2050.[^cite_note-447]&#x20;

### Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_the_United_States)



Interchange between [Interstate 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_10) and [Interstate 45](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_45) in [Houston](13774.md), Texas

The United States' 4 million miles (6.4 million kilometers) of road network, owned almost entirely by state and local governments, is the [longest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_road_network_size) in the world.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449] The extensive [Interstate Highway System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Highway_System) that connects all major U.S. cities is funded mostly by the federal government but maintained by [state departments of transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_and_insular_area_departments_of_transportation). The system is further extended by [state highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_highway#United_States) and some private [toll roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toll_road).&#x20;

The U.S. ranks among the ten countries with the [highest vehicle ownership per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_vehicles_per_capita), with 850 vehicles per 1,000 residents in 2022. A 2024 survey reported 58.3% of households with two or more vehicles, and 21.9% with three or more.[^cite_note-450] According to the 2022 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey), 68.7% of U.S. workers aged 16 and older drove alone to work, 2.9% walked or cycled, and 3.1% used public transportation.[^cite_note-Review-Commuting2022-451]&#x20;

[Public transportation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transportation_in_the_United_States) is well developed in the largest urban areas, notably New York City, Washington, D.C., Boston, Philadelphia, Chicago, and the San Francisco Bay Area; otherwise, coverage is generally less extensive than in most other developed countries. The U.S. also has many relatively [car-dependent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car-dependent) localities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlack1995-452]&#x20;

Long-distance intercity travel is provided primarily by airlines, but travel by rail is more common along the [Northeast Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Corridor), where the only [high-speed rail in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail_in_the_United_States) that meets international standards operates. [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak), the country's government-sponsored national passenger rail company, has a relatively sparse network compared to that of Western European countries.[^cite_note-453] Service is concentrated in the Northeast, California, the Midwest, and the Pacific Northwest.[^cite_note-454] [The country's rail transport network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_the_United_States), the [longest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_rail_transport_network_size) in the world at 182,412.3 mi (293,564.2 km),[^cite_note-455] handles mostly [freight](28362.md)[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457] (in contrast to more passenger-centered rail in Europe[^cite_note-458]). Because they are often privately owned, U.S. freight railroads lag behind much of the rest of the world in terms of electrification.[^cite_note-459]&#x20;



[Hartsfield–Jackson Atlanta International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartsfield%E2%80%93Jackson_Atlanta_International_Airport), serving the [Atlanta metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_metropolitan_area), is the world's [busiest airport by passenger traffic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_airports_by_passenger_traffic) with over 75 million passengers as of 2021.

[^cite_note-BusyAirport-460]

[^cite_note-461]

The United States has an extensive air transportation network. [U.S. civilian airlines](17111897.md) are all privately owned. The three largest airlines in the world, by total number of passengers carried, are U.S.-based; [American Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines) became the global leader after its 2013 merger with [US Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Airways).[^cite_note-462] Of the 50 busiest airports in the world, 16 are in the United States, as well as five of the top 10.[^cite_note-PANYNJ_2021_report-463] The world's busiest airport by passenger volume is [Hartsfield–Jackson Atlanta International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartsfield%E2%80%93Jackson_Atlanta_International_Airport) in [Atlanta](3138.md), Georgia.[^cite_note-BusyAirport-460][^cite_note-PANYNJ_2021_report-463] In 2022, most of the [19,969 U.S. airports](4718801.md)[^cite_note-464] were owned and operated by local government authorities, and there are also some private airports. Some 5,193 are designated as "public use", including for [general aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_aviation). The [Transportation Security Administration](172933.md) (TSA) has provided security at most major airports since 2001.&#x20;

[The country's inland waterways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inland_waterways_of_the_United_States) are the world's [fifth-longest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_waterways_length), totaling 25,482 mi (41,009 km).[^cite_note-465] They are used extensively for freight, recreation, and a small amount of passenger traffic. Of the world's [50 busiest container ports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_container_ports), four are located in the United States, with the busiest in the country being the [Port of Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Los_Angeles).[^cite_note-466]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_United_States)

### Population

Main articles: [Americans](19792942.md) and [Race and ethnicity in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_ethnicity_in_the_United_States)

See also: [List of U.S. states by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_population)

| State | Population (millions) |

| [Pennsylvania](23332.md) | 13.1 |

The [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) reported 331,449,281 residents on April 1, 2020,[^cite_note-468][^cite_note-2020CENSUS-469] making the United States the [third-most-populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) in the world, after India and China.[^cite_note-470] The Census Bureau's official 2025 population estimate was 341,784,857, an increase of 3.1% since the 2020 census.[^cite_note-Vintage2025-22] According to the Bureau's [U.S. Population Clock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._and_World_Population_Clock), on July 1, 2024, the U.S. population had a net gain of one person every 16 seconds, or about 5400 people per day.[^cite_note-471] In 2023, 51% of Americans age 15 and over were married, 6% were [widowed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widowed), 10% were divorced, and 34% had never been married.[^cite_note-472] In 2023, the [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) for the U.S. stood at 1.6 children per woman,[^cite_note-473] and, at 23%, it had the world's highest rate of children living in [single-parent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_parents_in_the_United_States) households in 2019.[^cite_note-474] Most Americans live in the [suburbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburb) of major metropolitan areas.&#x20;

The United States has a diverse population; 37 [ancestry groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_ancestries) have more than one million members.[^cite_note-An2000-475] [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_whites) with ancestry from Europe, the Middle East, or North Africa form the largest [racial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_\(human_classification\)) and [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group) at 57.8% of the United States population.[^cite_note-476][^cite_note-477] [Hispanic and Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) form the second-largest group and are 18.7% of the United States population. [African Americans](2154.md) constitute the country's third-largest ancestry group and are 12.1% of the total U.S. population.[^cite_note-An2000-475] Asian Americans are the country's fourth-largest group, composing 5.9% of the United States population. The country's 3.7 million Native Americans account for about 1%,[^cite_note-An2000-475] and some 574 native tribes are recognized by the federal government.[^cite_note-478] In 2024, the [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_age) of the United States population was 39.1 years.[^cite_note-479]&#x20;

### Urbanization

Main articles: [Urbanization in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization_in_the_United_States) and [List of United States cities by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population)

Houses near farmland

Most Americans live in [suburbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburb) and [exurbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exurb), such as [Monroe Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Township,_Middlesex_County,_New_Jersey), [New Jersey](21648.md), in the [New York metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area).

About 82% of Americans live in [metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_statistical_area), particularly in [suburbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburb) and outer-ring [exurbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exurb);[^cite_note-CIA-2018-383] about half of those reside in cities with populations over 50,000.[^cite_note-480] In 2024, 346 [incorporated U.S. municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population#50_states_and_District_of_Columbia) had populations over 100,000, 11 cities had more than one million residents, and four cities—[New York City](645042.md), [Los Angeles](18110.md), [Chicago](6886.md), and [Houston](13774.md)—had populations exceeding two million.[^cite_note-481] Some 56 U.S. metropolitan areas [have one million or more residents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_statistical_areas#United_States).&#x20;

More recently, the fastest-growing metropolitan areas were in the South, while southern metros along the Mexican border and Gulf Coast metros susceptible to hurricanes declined the most in 2025.[^cite_note-CenPopGrowthSlowing-482] The [New York metro area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metro_area), which gained the most new residents in 2024, fell to 13th in 2025, due to a fall in immigrants.[^cite_note-CenPopGrowthSlowing-482] The top metro areas with rising populations in 2025 were [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_metropolitan_area) and [Dallas–Fort Worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Metroplex), followed by [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_metropolitan_area), [Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_metropolitan_area) and [Charlotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-CenPopGrowthSlowing-482]&#x20;
