### Language

Main article: [Languages of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_United_States)



Most spoken languages in the U.S.

While many languages and [dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialects) are spoken in the United States, [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_English) is by far the most commonly spoken and written.[^cite_note-484] English was designated the [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) of the United States by [Executive Order 14224](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_14224) in March 2025.[^cite_note-Review-EnglishEO-485][^cite_note-EOWP-6] However, the U.S. has never had a statutory official language, as Congress has never passed a law to designate English as official for all three federal branches. Some laws, such as [U.S. naturalization requirements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalized_citizen_of_the_United_States), nonetheless standardize English. Twenty-eight states and the [United States Virgin Islands](32135.md) have laws that designate English as the sole official language; 19 states and the [District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia) have no official language.[^cite_note-486] Three states and four U.S. territories have recognized local or indigenous languages in addition to English: Hawaii ([Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language)),[^cite_note-487] Alaska ([twenty Native languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native_languages)),[^cite_note-488][^cite_note-489] South Dakota ([Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sioux_language)),[^cite_note-LakotaCommon-490] American Samoa ([Samoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoan_language)), Puerto Rico ([Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rican_Spanish)), Guam ([Chamorro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamorro_language)), and the Northern Mariana Islands ([Carolinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolinian_language) and Chamorro). In total, 169 Native American languages are spoken in the United States.[^cite_note-491] In Puerto Rico, Spanish is more widely spoken than English.[^cite_note-PuertoRicoTranslation-492]&#x20;

According to the [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey) (2020),[^cite_note-ACS2021-493] some 245.4 million people in the U.S. age five and older spoke only English at home. About 41.2 million spoke [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language_in_the_United_States) at home, making it the second most commonly used language. Other languages spoken at home by one million people or more include [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language_in_the_United_States) (3.40 million), [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language_in_the_United_States) (1.71 million), [Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_language_in_the_United_States) (1.52 million), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language_in_the_United_States) (1.39 million), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language_in_the_United_States) (1.18 million), [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language_in_the_United_States) (1.07 million), and [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language_in_the_United_States) (1.04 million). [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language_in_the_United_States), spoken by 1 million people at home in 2010, fell to 881,000 estimated total speakers in 2020.[^cite_note-494]&#x20;

### Immigration

Main articles: [Immigration to the United States](15051.md) and [History of immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_immigration_to_the_United_States)



The [Mexico–United States border wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall) between [San Diego](28504.md) (left) and [Tijuana](101739.md) (right)

America's immigrant population is by far the world's [largest in absolute terms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_by_immigrant_and_emigrant_population).[^cite_note-UNdef-495][^cite_note-496] In 2022, there were 87.7 million immigrants and [U.S.-born children of immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second-generation_immigrants_in_the_United_States) in the United States, accounting for nearly 27% of the overall U.S. population.[^cite_note-497] In 2017, out of the U.S. foreign-born population, some 45% (20.7 million) were naturalized citizens, 27% (12.3 million) were lawful permanent residents, 6% (2.2 million) were temporary lawful residents, and 23% (10.5 million) were [unauthorized immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undocumented_immigrant_population_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-KeyFindings-498] In 2019, the top countries of origin for immigrants were Mexico (24% of immigrants), India (6%), China (5%), the Philippines (4.5%), and El Salvador (3%).[^cite_note-499] In fiscal year 2022, over one million immigrants (most of whom entered through [family reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chain_migration#Legislation)) were granted [legal residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_residence_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-500] The undocumented immigrant population in the U.S. reached a record high of 14 million in 2023.[^cite_note-501]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_United_States)

Religious affiliation in the U.S., according to a 2025 [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.) poll:

[^cite_note-Gallup-14]

1. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_the_United_States) (32%)

2. [Catholicism](410868.md) (20%)

3. [Non-specific Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_United_States) (12%)

4. [Mormonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormonism_in_the_United_States) (1%)

5. [Judaism](1131183.md) (2%)

6. Other religion (6%)

7. [Unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_the_United_States) (24%)

8. Unanswered (3%)

The [First Amendment](31653.md) guarantees the [free exercise of religion in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Exercise_Clause) and forbids Congress from passing laws respecting [its establishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Establishment_Clause).[^cite_note-Donadio-2021-502][^cite_note-503] Religious practice is widespread, among the [most diverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_ethnic_and_cultural_diversity_level) in the world,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlesina2003-504] and profoundly vibrant.[^cite_note-pewreligion-505]&#x20;

The country has the world's [largest Christian population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_by_country), which includes the [fourth-largest population of Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_by_country).[^cite_note-Global_Christianity-506] Other notable faiths include [Judaism](15624.md), [Buddhism](3267529.md), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), [Islam](6037917.md), [New Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Age), and [Native American religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_religions).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESewell2010-507] Religious practice varies significantly by region.[^cite_note-Williams-2023-508] "[Ceremonial deism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceremonial_deism)" is common in American culture.[^cite_note-509]&#x20;

The overwhelming majority of [Americans](19792942.md) believe in a [higher power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_Power) or spiritual force, engage in [spiritual practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_practice) such as prayer, and consider themselves religious or [spiritual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality).[^cite_note-Kallo-2023-510][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFroeseUecker2022-511] In the Southern United States' "[Bible Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Belt)", [evangelical Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism) plays a significant role culturally; [New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England) and the Western United States [tend to be more secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unchurched_Belt).[^cite_note-Williams-2023-508][^cite_note-512] [Mormonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormonism), a [Restorationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restorationism) movement founded in the U.S. in 1830,[^cite_note-Review-MormonOrigins-513][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2008727–728-514] is the predominant religion in Utah and a major religion in Idaho.&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in the United States](15982224.md)

Further information: [Healthcare in the United States](27553159.md) and [Health insurance in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_in_the_United_States)

See also: [History of public health in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_public_health_in_the_United_States) and [History of medicine in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_medicine_in_the_United_States)



The [Texas Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Medical_Center) in [Houston](13774.md) is the largest medical complex in the world.

[^cite_note-515]

[^cite_note-516]

&#x20;In 2018, it employed 120,000 people and treated 10 million patients.

[^cite_note-517]

According to the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) (CDC), average U.S. life expectancy at birth reached 79.0 years in 2024, its highest recorded level and an increase of 0.6 years over 2023. The CDC attributed the improvement mainly to lower mortality from unintentional injuries, COVID-19, heart disease, [cancer](105219.md), and homicide. Heart disease, cancer, and unintentional injuries remained the leading causes of death.[^cite_note-518] In 2024, life expectancy at birth for American men rose to 76.5 years (+0.7 years compared to 2023), while life expectancy for women was 81.4 years (+0.3 years).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEXuMurphyKochanekArias2026-519] Starting in 1998, life expectancy in the U.S. fell [behind that of other wealthy industrialized countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy), and Americans' "health disadvantage" gap has been increasing ever since.[^cite_note-520]&#x20;

The Commonwealth Fund reported in 2020 that the U.S. had the [highest suicide rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_suicide_rate) among the 11 [high-income countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_countries) included in its study.[^cite_note-521] [Obesity affected 40.3% of U.S. adults aged 20 and older](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity_in_the_United_States) in August 2021–August 2023.[^cite_note-Review-Obesity2024-522] The U.S. healthcare system far [outspends that of any other country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_total_health_expenditure_\(PPP\)_per_capita), measured both in per capita spending and as a percentage of GDP, but attains worse healthcare outcomes when compared to peer countries for reasons that are debated.[^cite_note-523] The United States is the only developed country [without a system of universal healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_reform_in_the_United_States), and [a significant proportion of the population that does not carry health insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_coverage_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVladeck2003-524] Government-funded healthcare coverage for the poor ([Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid)) and for those age 65 and older ([Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\))) is available to Americans who meet the programs' income or age qualifications. In 2010, President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) signed the [Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act) into law.[^cite_note-525][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOberlander2010-526] Since the 2022 U.S. Supreme Court decision *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women%27s_Health_Organization)*, which effectively overruled *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* (1973), [abortion in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_States) is no longer federally protected but is subject to the laws of each state or territory.[^cite_note-527][^cite_note-528]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_United_States)

Photograph of the University of Virginia

Some 77% of American college students attend public institutions

[^cite_note-529]

&#x20;such as the [University of Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Virginia), founded by [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) in 1819.

American primary and secondary education, known in the U.S. as [K–12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%E2%80%9312_education_in_the_United_States) ("kindergarten through 12th grade"), is decentralized. School systems are operated by state, territorial, and sometimes municipal governments and regulated by the [U.S. Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Education). In general, children are required to attend school or [an approved homeschool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeschooling_in_the_United_States) from the age of five or six ([kindergarten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kindergarten) or [first grade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_grade)) until they are 18 years old. This often brings students through the [12th grade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelfth_grade), the final year of a U.S. high school, but some states and territories allow them to leave school earlier, at age 16 or 17.[^cite_note-530] The U.S. spends more on education per student than any other country,[^cite_note-531] an average of \$18,614 per year per public elementary and secondary school student in 2020–2021.[^cite_note-532] Among Americans age 25 and older, 92.2% graduated from high school, 62.7% attended some college, 37.7% earned a [bachelor's degree](188874.md), and 14.2% earned a graduate degree.[^cite_note-533] The [U.S. literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_in_the_United_States) is near-universal.[^cite_note-CIA-2018-383][^cite_note-534] The U.S. has produced the [most Nobel Prize winners of any country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nobel_laureates_by_country), with [411](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_American_Nobel_laureates) (having won 413 awards).[^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536]&#x20;

[U.S. tertiary or higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_the_United_States) has earned a global reputation. Many of the world's top universities, as listed by various ranking organizations, are in the United States, including 19 of the top 25.[^cite_note-537][^cite_note-538] American higher education is dominated by [state university systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_university_system), although [the country's many private universities and colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_universities_in_the_United_States) enroll about 20% of all American students. Local [community colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_colleges_in_the_United_States) generally offer [open admissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_admissions), lower tuition, and coursework leading to an [associate degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associate_degree) or a [non-degree certificate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certificate_program).[^cite_note-539]&#x20;

As for [public expenditures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_expenditure) on higher education, the U.S. spends more per student than the [OECD](33853117.md) average, and Americans spend more than all nations in combined public and private spending.[^cite_note-education_spending-540] Colleges and universities directly funded by the federal government do not charge tuition and are limited to military personnel and government employees, including: the [U.S. service academies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_service_academies), the [Naval Postgraduate School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Postgraduate_School), and [military staff colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_military_staff_colleges). Despite some student [loan forgiveness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loan_forgiveness) programs in place,[^cite_note-541] [student loan debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_debt) increased by 102% between 2010 and 2020,[^cite_note-542] and exceeded \$1.7 trillion in 2022.[^cite_note-543]&#x20;

## Culture and society

Main articles: [Culture of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States) and [Society of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_of_the_United_States)

See also: [Human rights in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_the_United_States)

The Statue of Liberty, a large teal bronze sculpture on a stone pedestal

The [Statue of Liberty](28617.md) (*Liberty Enlightening the World*) on [Liberty Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Island) in [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor) was an 1886 gift from France that has become an iconic symbol of the [American Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Dream).

[^cite_note-544]

The United States is home to [a wide variety of ethnic groups, traditions, and customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-545][^cite_note-546] The country has been described as having [the values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americanism_\(ideology\)) of [individualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individualism) and [personal autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-libertarianism),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrabbBaerCurtis1999-547][^cite_note-548] as well as a strong [work ethic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Work_ethic)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPorter2010-549] and [competitiveness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Competition).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStephens1952-550] Voluntary [altruism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altruism) toward others also plays a major role;[^cite_note-551][^cite_note-552][^cite_note-553] according to a 2016 study by the [Charities Aid Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charities_Aid_Foundation), Americans donated 1.44% of total GDP to charity—the [highest rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_charitable_donation) in the world by a large margin.[^cite_note-554] Americans have traditionally [been characterized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotypes_of_Americans) by a unifying political belief in an "[American Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Creed)" that emphasizes [consent of the governed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_of_the_governed), [liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty), [equality under the law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_under_the_law), [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), [social equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_equality), [property rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_rights), and a preference for [limited government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited_government).[^cite_note-555][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuntington2004-556][^cite_note-557] The U.S. has acquired significant [hard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_power) and [soft power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_power) through [its diplomatic influence](24296062.md), [economic power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_power), [military alliances](250766.md), and [cultural exports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_exports) such as [American movies](418334.md), [music](171080.md), [video games](31669618.md), [sports](2257795.md), and [food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_cuisine).[^cite_note-558][^cite_note-559] The influence that the United States exerts on other countries through soft power is referred to as [Americanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americanization).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerghahn2010-560]&#x20;

Nearly all present Americans or their ancestors came from [Europe, Africa, or Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Eurasia) (the "[Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World)") within the past five centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiorinaPeterson201097-561] [Mainstream](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/mainstream) American culture is a [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture) largely derived from the [traditions of European immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_American#Culture) with influences from many other sources, such as [traditions brought by slaves from Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_culture).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolloway200518–38-562][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson2000116-563] More recent immigration from [Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American) and especially [Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_culture) has added to a cultural mix that has been described as a homogenizing [melting pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_pot), and a heterogeneous [salad bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salad_bowl_\(cultural_idea\)), with immigrants contributing to, and often [assimilating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assimilation_\(phonology\)) into, mainstream American culture.&#x20;

Under the [First Amendment to the Constitution](31653.md), the United States is considered to have the [strongest protections of free speech of any country](634892.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeman201310,_201-564] [Flag desecration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_desecration_in_the_United_States), [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech_in_the_United_States), [blasphemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemy_law_in_the_United_States), and [lese majesty](934323.md) are all forms of protected expression.[^cite_note-565][^cite_note-566][^cite_note-567] A 2016 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) poll found that Americans were the most supportive of free expression of any polity measured.[^cite_note-568] Additionally, they are the "most supportive of [freedom of the press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press_in_the_United_States) and the [right to use the Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_Internet_access) without government censorship".[^cite_note-569] The U.S. is a [socially progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_liberalism) country[^cite_note-570] with [permissive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permissive_society) attitudes surrounding [human sexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_sexuality).[^cite_note-Derks-2020-571] [LGBTQ rights in the United States](3510770.md) are among the most advanced by global standards.[^cite_note-Derks-2020-571][^cite_note-572][^cite_note-573]&#x20;

The [American Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Dream), or the perception that Americans enjoy high levels of [social mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socio-economic_mobility_in_the_United_States), plays a key role in attracting immigrants.[^cite_note-574][^cite_note-575] Whether this perception is accurate has been a topic of debate.[^cite_note-socialmobility-576][^cite_note-CAP-577][^cite_note-578] While mainstream culture holds that the United States is a [classless society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classless_society),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutfeld200265-579] scholars identify significant differences between [the country's social classes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class_in_the_United_States), affecting [socialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialization), language, and values.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZweig2004-580][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoff-Ginsberg1989-581] Americans tend to greatly value [socioeconomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socioeconomics) achievement, but [being ordinary or average](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Average_Joe) is promoted by some as a noble condition as well.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Keefe2005-582]&#x20;

The [National Foundation on the Arts and the Humanities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Foundation_on_the_Arts_and_the_Humanities) is an agency of the United States federal government that was established in 1965 with the purpose to "develop and promote a broadly conceived national policy of support for the humanities and the arts in the United States, and for institutions which preserve the cultural heritage of the United States."[^cite_note-583]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [American literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_literature)

See also: [American philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_philosophy)

Photograph of Mark Twain

[Mark Twain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain), whom [William Faulkner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Faulkner) called "the father of American literature"

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJelliffe1956-584]

Colonial American authors were influenced by [John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke) and other [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) philosophers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine2013157–159-585][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELauter1994a503–509-586] The [American Revolutionary Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) (1765–1783) is notable for the political writings of [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin), [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton), [Thomas Paine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Paine), and [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson). Shortly before and after the [Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War), the newspaper rose to prominence, filling a demand for anti-British national literature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine2013163-587][^cite_note-588] An early novel is [William Hill Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hill_Brown)'s *[The Power of Sympathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Power_of_Sympathy)*, published in 1789.[^cite_note-Review-Brown1789-589] Writer and critic [John Neal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Neal) in the early- to mid-19th century helped advance America toward a unique literature and culture by criticizing predecessors such as [Washington Irving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Irving) for imitating their British counterparts, and by influencing writers such as [Edgar Allan Poe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Allan_Poe),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELease197280-590] who took American poetry and short fiction in new directions. [Ralph Waldo Emerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Waldo_Emerson) and [Margaret Fuller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Fuller) pioneered the influential [Transcendentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcendentalism) movement;[^cite_note-591][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoviello2005-592] [Henry David Thoreau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_David_Thoreau), author of *[Walden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walden)*, was influenced by this movement.&#x20;

The conflict surrounding [abolitionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States) inspired writers, like [Harriet Beecher Stowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harriet_Beecher_Stowe), and authors of slave narratives, such as [Frederick Douglass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass). [Nathaniel Hawthorne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Hawthorne)'s *[The Scarlet Letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Scarlet_Letter)* (1850) explored the dark side of American history, as did [Herman Melville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Melville)'s *[Moby-Dick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moby-Dick)* (1851). Major American poets of the 19th century [American Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Renaissance_\(literature\)) include [Walt Whitman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Whitman), Melville, and [Emily Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_Dickinson).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine2013444–447-593][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELauter1994a1228,_1233,_1260-594] [Mark Twain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain) was the first major American writer to be born in the West. [Henry James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_James) achieved international recognition with novels like *[The Portrait of a Lady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Portrait_of_a_Lady)* (1881). As literacy rates rose, periodicals published more stories centered around industrial workers, women, and the rural poor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine20131269–1270-595][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELauter1994b8–10-596] [Naturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalism_\(literature\)), [regionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_literary_regionalism), and [realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_realism#UnitedStates) were the major literary movements of the period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine20131271–1273-597][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELauter1994b12-598]&#x20;

While [modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_modernism) generally took on an international character, modernist authors working within the United States more often rooted their work in specific regions, peoples, and cultures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine20131850–1851-599] Following the Great Migration to northern cities, African-American and black [West Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indian_Americans) authors of the [Harlem Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem_Renaissance) developed an independent tradition of literature that rebuked a history of inequality and celebrated black culture. An important cultural export during the [Jazz Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_Age), these writings were a key influence on *[Négritude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%A9gritude)*, a philosophy emerging in the 1930s among francophone writers of the [African diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora).[^cite_note-600][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhilipson2006-601] In the 1950s, an ideal of homogeneity led many authors to attempt to write the [Great American Novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_American_Novel),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine20132260–2261-602] while the [Beat Generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_Generation) rejected this conformity, using styles that elevated the impact of the [spoken word](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spoken_word) over mechanics to describe drug use, sexuality, and the failings of society.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine20132262-603][^cite_note-604] Contemporary literature is more pluralistic than in previous eras, with the closest thing to a unifying feature being a trend toward self-conscious [experiments with language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_literature).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaymLevine20132266–2267-605] Twelve American laureates have won the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md).[^cite_note-606]&#x20;

### Mass media

Main article: [Mass media in the United States](158546.md)

See also: [Newspapers in the United States](9030472.md), [Television in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_the_United_States), [Broadcasting in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting_in_the_United_States), [Public broadcasting in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_broadcasting_in_the_United_States), [Internet in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_in_the_United_States), [Radio in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_in_the_United_States), and [Video games in the United States](31669618.md)



[Comcast Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast_Center) in [Philadelphia](50585.md), headquarters of [Comcast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast), one of the world's [largest telecommunications companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_telecommunications_companies) and media conglomerates

The four major broadcasters in the U.S. are the [National Broadcasting Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Broadcasting_Company) (NBC), [Columbia Broadcasting System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Broadcasting_System) (CBS), [American Broadcasting Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company) (ABC), and [Fox Broadcasting Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Broadcasting_Company) (Fox). The four major broadcast television networks are all commercial entities. The [Public Broadcasting Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBS) (PBS) is the country's major non-commercial [public broadcast network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_broadcaster);[^cite_note-About_PBS-607] it also provides [educational programming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_television) through local PBS stations.[^cite_note-608][^cite_note-609][^cite_note-610][^cite_note-611] The [U.S. cable television system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_television_in_the_United_States) offers hundreds of channels catering to a variety of niches.[^cite_note-612] In 2021, about 83% of Americans over age 12 listened to [broadcast radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_broadcasting), while about 40% listened to [podcasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Podcast).[^cite_note-613] In the prior year, there were 15,460 licensed full-power radio stations in the U.S. according to the [Federal Communications Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Communications_Commission) (FCC).[^cite_note-614] Public radio broadcasting is largely supplied by [National Public Radio](29697232.md) (NPR), incorporated in February 1970 under the [Public Broadcasting Act of 1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Broadcasting_Act_of_1967).[^cite_note-615]&#x20;

U.S. newspapers with a global reach and reputation include *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*, *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, and *[USA Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Today)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShaffer2006116-616] [About 800 publications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Spanish-language_newspapers_published_in_the_United_States) are produced in Spanish.[^cite_note-617][^cite_note-618] With few exceptions, newspapers are privately owned, either by large chains such as [Gannett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gannett_Company) or [McClatchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_McClatchy_Company), which own dozens or even hundreds of newspapers; by small chains that own a handful of papers; or, in an increasingly rare situation, by individuals or families. Major cities often have [alternative newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_newspaper) to complement the mainstream daily papers, such as *[The Village Voice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Village_Voice)* in New York City and *[LA Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LA_Weekly)* in Los Angeles. The five [most-visited websites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-visited_websites) in the world are [Google](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Search), [YouTube](3524766.md), [Facebook](7529378.md), [Instagram](31591547.md), and [ChatGPT](72417803.md)—all of them American-owned. Other popular platforms used include [X](9988187.md) (formerly Twitter) and [Amazon](90451.md).[^cite_note-alexa-topsitesus-619][^cite_note-620] In 2025, the U.S. was the world's [second-largest video game market by revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_video_games_markets_by_country) (after China).[^cite_note-621] In 2015, the U.S. video game industry consisted of 2,457 companies that employed around 220,000 jobs and generated \$30.4 billion in revenue.[^cite_note-\:0-622] There are 444 game publishers, developers, and hardware companies in California alone.[^cite_note-623] In the [Game Developers Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_Developers_Conference) (GDC) 2025 survey, 58% of responding [game developers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_game_developer) were based in the U.S.[^cite_note-624]&#x20;

[Media freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_the_United_States) was classified as "problematic" by [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) in 2026, and scholars have noted a significant rise in [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship) and [self-censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-censorship) in recent years.[^cite_note-\:32-625]&#x20;

### Theater

Main article: [Theater in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_in_the_United_States)



[Broadway theaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadway_theater) in [Theater District, Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_District,_Manhattan)

The United States is well known for its theater. Mainstream theater in the United States derives from the old European theatrical tradition and has been heavily influenced by the [British theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaxon2011-626] By the middle of the 19th century, America had created new distinct dramatic forms in the [Tom Shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Shows), the [showboat theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Showboat) and the [minstrel show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minstrel_show).[^cite_note-627] The central hub of the American theater scene is the [Theater District in Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_District,_Manhattan), with its divisions of [Broadway](725252.md), [off-Broadway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off-Broadway), and [off-off-Broadway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off-off-Broadway).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELondréWatermeier1998-628]&#x20;

Many movie and television [celebrities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celebrity) have gotten their big break working in New York productions. Outside New York City, many cities have professional [regional or resident theater companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_theater_in_the_United_States) that produce their own seasons. The biggest-budget theatrical productions are musicals. U.S. theater has an active [community theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_theater) culture.[^cite_note-629]&#x20;

The [Tony Awards](54741.md) recognize excellence in live Broadway theater and are presented at an annual ceremony in [Manhattan](45470.md). The awards are given for Broadway productions and performances. One is also given for [regional theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_theatre_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-630]&#x20;

### Visual arts

Main articles: [Visual art of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_art_of_the_United_States) and [Architecture of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_the_United_States)



*[American Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Gothic)* (1930) by [Grant Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grant_Wood) is one of the most famous [American paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_art_of_the_United_States) and is widely [parodied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parody).

[^cite_note-BBC-631]

[Folk art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_art_of_the_United_States) in [colonial America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_america) grew out of artisanal [craftsmanship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workmanship) in communities that allowed commonly trained people to individually express themselves. It was distinct from Europe's tradition of [high art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_culture), which was less accessible and generally less relevant to early American settlers.[^cite_note-632] Cultural movements in art and craftsmanship in colonial America generally lagged behind those of Western Europe. For example, the prevailing medieval style of [woodworking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodworking) and primitive [sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sculpture_of_the_United_States) became integral to early American folk art, despite the emergence of [Renaissance styles](311401.md) in England in the late 16th and early 17th centuries. The new English styles would have been early enough to make a considerable impact on American folk art, but American styles and forms had already been firmly adopted. Not only did styles change slowly in early America, but there was a tendency for rural artisans there to continue their traditional forms longer than their urban counterparts did—and far longer than those in Western Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColeman201310,_201-564]&#x20;

The [Hudson River School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River_School) was a mid-19th-century movement in the visual arts tradition of European [naturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)). The 1913 [Armory Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armory_Show) in New York City, an exhibition of European [modernist art](163543.md), shocked the public and transformed the U.S. art scene.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1988-633]&#x20;

[American Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Realism) and [American Regionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Regionalism) sought to reflect and give America new ways of looking at itself. [Georgia O'Keeffe](311615.md), [Marsden Hartley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsden_Hartley), and others experimented with new and individualistic styles, which would become known as [American modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_modernism). Major artistic movements such as the [abstract expressionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_expressionism) of [Jackson Pollock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_Pollock) and [Willem de Kooning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willem_de_Kooning) and the [pop art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_art) of [Andy Warhol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Warhol) and [Roy Lichtenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Lichtenstein) developed largely in the United States. Major photographers include [Alfred Stieglitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Stieglitz), [Edward Steichen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Steichen), [Dorothea Lange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorothea_Lange), [Edward Weston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Weston), [James Van Der Zee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Van_Der_Zee), [Ansel Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansel_Adams), and [Gordon Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Parks).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavenport199167-634]&#x20;

The tide of [modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) and then [postmodernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodernism) has brought global fame to American architects, including [Frank Lloyd Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Lloyd_Wright), [Philip Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Johnson), and [Frank Gehry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Gehry).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJansonJanson2003955-635] The [Metropolitan Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Museum_of_Art) in [Manhattan](45470.md) is the largest [art museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_museum) in the United States[^cite_note-METLargestArtMuseum-636] and the [fourth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_art_museums) in the world.[^cite_note-637]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of the United States](171080.md)



The [Country Music Hall of Fame and Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_Music_Hall_of_Fame_and_Museum) in [Nashville, Tennessee](22018.md)

[American folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_folk_music) encompasses numerous music genres, variously known as traditional music, traditional [folk music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music), contemporary folk music, or roots music. Many traditional songs have been sung within the same family or folk group for generations, and sometimes trace back to such origins as the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles), [mainland Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Europe), or [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_music).[^cite_note-afc-638] The rhythmic and lyrical styles of African-American music in particular have influenced American music.[^cite_note-639] [Banjos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banjo) were brought to America through the slave trade. [Minstrel shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minstrel_show) incorporating the instrument into their acts led to its increased popularity and widespread production in the 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWinans1976-640][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShi2016378-641] The [electric guitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_guitar), first invented in the 1930s, and mass-produced by the 1940s, had an enormous influence on popular music, in particular due to the development of [rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_and_roll).[^cite_note-axe-642] The [synthesizer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthesizer), [turntablism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turntablism), and [electronic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_music) were also largely developed in the U.S.&#x20;

Elements from folk idioms such as the [blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues) and [old-time music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-time_music) were adopted and transformed into [popular genres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music) with global audiences. [Jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) grew from blues and [ragtime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ragtime) in the early 20th century, developing from the innovations and recordings of composers such as [W.C. Handy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W.C._Handy) and [Jelly Roll Morton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jelly_Roll_Morton). [Louis Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Armstrong) and [Duke Ellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Ellington) increased its popularity early in the 20th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiddle2001\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswhatwashotroller00biddpage_ix\]-643] [Country music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music) developed in the 1920s,[^cite_note-644] [bluegrass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluegrass_music)[^cite_note-645] and [rhythm and blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythm_and_blues) in the 1940s,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECorbettJanssenLundPfannestiel2014\[httpsopenstaxorgbooksus-historypages28-4-popular-culture-and-mass-media_28.4\]-646] and rock and roll in the 1950s.[^cite_note-axe-642] In the 1960s, [Bob Dylan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Dylan) emerged from the [folk revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_folk_music_revival) to become one of the country's most celebrated songwriters.[^cite_note-647] The musical forms of [punk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_rock) and [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop) both originated in the United States in the 1970s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunk2016-648]&#x20;

The United States has the world's [largest music market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_recorded_music_markets), with a total retail value of \$15.9 billion in 2022,[^cite_note-649] and is the largest exporter of music.[^cite_note-650][^cite_note-651] Most of the world's [major record companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Record_label#Major_labels) are based in the U.S.; they are represented by the [Recording Industry Association of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recording_Industry_Association_of_America) (RIAA).[^cite_note-652] Mid-20th-century American pop stars, such as [Frank Sinatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Sinatra)[^cite_note-653] and [Elvis Presley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elvis_Presley),[^cite_note-654] became [global celebrities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superstar) and [best-selling music artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_music_artists),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiddle2001\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswhatwashotroller00biddpage_ix\]-643] as have artists of the late 20th century, such as [Michael Jackson](14995351.md),[^cite_note-RIAA-655] [Madonna](142056.md),[^cite_note-656] [Whitney Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitney_Houston),[^cite_note-Rolling_Stone-2023-657] and [Mariah Carey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariah_Carey),[^cite_note-658] and of the early 21st century, such as [Eminem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminem),[^cite_note-659] [Britney Spears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britney_Spears),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdmondson2013490-660] [Lady Gaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_Gaga),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdmondson2013490-660] [Katy Perry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katy_Perry),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdmondson2013490-660] [Taylor Swift](5422144.md) and [Beyoncé](83688.md).[^cite_note-661]&#x20;

### Fashion

Main article: [Fashion in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_in_the_United_States)



*[Haute couture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haute_couture)* [fashion models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_model) on the [catwalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catwalk) during [New York Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Fashion_Week)

The United States has the world's largest [apparel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparel) market by revenue.[^cite_note-662] Apart from professional [business attire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_attire), American fashion is eclectic and predominantly informal. Americans' diverse cultural roots are reflected in their clothing; however, [sneakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sneaker), [jeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeans), [T-shirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-shirts), and [baseball caps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_cap) are emblematic of American styles.[^cite_note-AmericanClassicFashion-663] New York, with [its Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Fashion_Week), is considered to be one of the "Big Four" global [fashion capitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_capital), along with [Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Fashion_Week), [Milan](18486693.md), and [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Fashion_Week). A study demonstrated that general proximity to [Manhattan's Garment District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garment_District,_Manhattan) has been synonymous with American fashion since its inception in the early 20th century.[^cite_note-GarmentDistrictNYCFashionSymbolUS-664]&#x20;

A number of well-known [designer labels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Designer_label), among them [Tommy Hilfiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Hilfiger_\(company\)), [Ralph Lauren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Lauren_Corporation), [Tom Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Ford_\(brand\)) and [Calvin Klein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvin_Klein_\(fashion_house\)), are headquartered in [Manhattan](45470.md).[^cite_note-665][^cite_note-666] Labels cater to [niche markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niche_market), such as preteens. [New York Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Fashion_Week) is one of the most influential fashion shows in the world, and is held twice each year in Manhattan;[^cite_note-USNYCFashionWeekGlobalIndustryTonesetter-667] the annual [Met Gala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Met_Gala), also in Manhattan, has been called the fashion world's "biggest night".[^cite_note-MetGalaFashion'sBiggestNight1-668][^cite_note-MetGalaFashion'sBiggestNight2-669]&#x20;

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of the United States](418334.md)



The [Hollywood Sign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood_Sign) in the [Hollywood Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood_Hills), often regarded as the symbol of the [American film industry](418334.md)

The U.S. film industry has [a worldwide influence and following](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Hollywood). [Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood,_Los_Angeles), a district in central Los Angeles, the nation's second-most populous city, is also [metonymous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metonymous) for the American filmmaking industry.[^cite_note-670][^cite_note-671][^cite_note-672] The [major film studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_film_studios) of the United States are the primary source of the [most commercially successful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest-grossing_films) movies selling the most tickets in the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKerrigan201018-673][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavisDickinsonPattiVillarejo2015299-674]&#x20;

Largely centered in the New York City region from its beginnings in the late 19th century through the first decades of the 20th century,[^cite_note-675][^cite_note-676][^cite_note-677][^cite_note-678] the U.S. film industry has since been primarily based in and around Hollywood. Nonetheless, American film companies have been subject to the forces of [globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization) in the 21st century, and an increasing number of films are made elsewhere.[^cite_note-679] The [Academy Awards](324.md), popularly known as "the Oscars", have been held annually by the [Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_Motion_Picture_Arts_and_Sciences) since 1929,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrowneHuber2004236-680] and the [Golden Globes](142724.md) have been held annually since January 1944.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKroon2014338-681]&#x20;

The industry peaked in what is commonly referred to as the "[Golden Age of Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Hollywood_cinema)", from the early sound period until the early 1960s,[^cite_note-682] with screen actors such as [John Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wayne) and [Marilyn Monroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marilyn_Monroe) becoming iconic figures.[^cite_note-683][^cite_note-684] In the 1970s, "[New Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Hollywood)", or the "Hollywood Renaissance",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreven201323-685] was defined by grittier films influenced by French and Italian realist pictures of the [post-war period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_World_War_II).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorrison199811-686] The 21st century has been marked by the rise of American [streaming platforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streaming_platform), which came to rival traditional cinema.[^cite_note-RE-687][^cite_note-688]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [American cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_cuisine)



A [Thanksgiving dinner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanksgiving_dinner) with [roast turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roast_turkey), [mashed potatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashed_potatoes), [pickles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickled_cucumber), [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn), [candied yams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candied_yams), [cranberry jelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cranberry_jelly), [shrimps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrimp_and_prawn_as_food), [stuffing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffing), [green peas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_peas), [deviled eggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deviled_eggs), [green salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_salad), and [apple sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_sauce)

Early settlers were introduced by Native Americans to foods such as [turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_as_food), [sweet potatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_potato), [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), [squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cucurbita), and [maple syrup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple_syrup). Of the most enduring and pervasive examples are variations of the native dish called [succotash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succotash). Early settlers and later immigrants combined these with foods they were familiar with, such as [wheat flour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat_flour),[^cite_note-Wheat-689] beef, and milk, to create a distinctive American cuisine.[^cite_note-690][^cite_note-691] [New World crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World_crops), especially [pumpkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pumpkin), corn, [potatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potatoes), and turkey as the main course are part of a shared national menu on [Thanksgiving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanksgiving_\(United_States\)), when many Americans prepare or purchase traditional dishes to celebrate the occasion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMintz1996134-692]&#x20;

Characteristic American dishes such as [apple pie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_pie), [fried chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fried_chicken), [doughnuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doughnut), [french fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fries), [macaroni and cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macaroni_and_cheese), [ice cream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_cream), [hamburgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburger), [hot dogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_dog), and [American pizza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pizza_in_the_United_States) derive from the recipes of various immigrant groups.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiner20011-693][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoe19995-694][^cite_note-695][^cite_note-696] [Mexican dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican-American_cuisine) such as [burritos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burritos) and [tacos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacos) preexisted the United States in areas later annexed from Mexico, and [adaptations of Chinese cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Chinese_cuisine) as well as [pasta dishes freely adapted from Italian sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian-American_cuisine) are all widely consumed.[^cite_note-IFT-697]&#x20;

American [chefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chef) have had a significant impact on society both domestically and internationally. In 1946, the [Culinary Institute of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culinary_Institute_of_America) was founded by [Katharine Angell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katharine_Cramer_Angell) and [Frances Roth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frances_Roth). This would become the United States' most prestigious culinary school, where many of the most talented American chefs would study prior to successful careers.[^cite_note-698][^cite_note-FTfbs-699] The [United States restaurant industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_restaurant_industry) was projected at \$899 billion in sales for 2020,[^cite_note-Brownfield-2020-700][^cite_note-Ramirez-701] and employed more than 15 million people, representing 10% of the nation's workforce directly.[^cite_note-Brownfield-2020-700] It is the country's second-largest private employer and the third-largest employer overall.[^cite_note-Noguchi-2020-702][^cite_note-703] The United States is home to over 220 [Michelin-starred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_star) restaurants, [14 of which were awarded three stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Michelin_3-star_restaurants_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-704]&#x20;

[Wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_wine) has been produced in what is now the United States since the 1500s, with the [first widespread production beginning in what is now New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_wine) in 1628.[^cite_note-705][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBirchellSteel2013-706][^cite_note-New_Mexico._Office_of_Cultural_Affairs_1995_p.-707] In the modern U.S., wine production is undertaken in all fifty states, with [California producing 84 percent of all U.S. wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_wine). With more than 1,100,000 acres (4,500 km<sup>2</sup>) under vine, the United States is the [fourth-largest wine-producing country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_wine_production) in the world, after [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_wine), [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_wine), and [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_wine).[^cite_note-Sotheby,_p._462-708][^cite_note-Oxford,_p._719-709]&#x20;

The [classic American diner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diner), a casual restaurant type originally intended for the working class, emerged during the 19th century from converted railroad [dining cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dining_car) made stationary. The diner soon evolved into purpose-built structures whose number expanded greatly in the 20th century.[^cite_note-710] The American [fast-food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast-food) industry developed alongside the nation's [car culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950s_American_automobile_culture).[^cite_note-711] American restaurants developed the [drive-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive-in) format in the 1920s, which they began to replace with the [drive-through](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive-through) format by the 1940s.[^cite_note-drivethru-712][^cite_note-713] American [fast-food restaurant](54923.md) chains, such as [McDonald's](2480627.md), [Burger King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burger_King), [Chick-fil-A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chick-fil-A), [Kentucky Fried Chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_Fried_Chicken), [Dunkin' Donuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunkin%27_Donuts) and [many others](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fast_food_restaurant_chains#United_States), have numerous outlets around the world.[^cite_note-Pavlova-2019-714]&#x20;

### Sports

Main articles: [Sports in the United States](2257795.md) and [United States at the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_at_the_Olympics)



A [collegiate-level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_football) game of [American football](18951490.md), the most popular sport in the U.S.

The most popular spectator sports in the U.S. are [American football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_football_in_the_United_States), [basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_in_the_United_States), [baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_in_the_United_States), [soccer](579105.md), and [ice hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_hockey_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-715] Their [premier leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_professional_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada) are, respectively, the [National Football League](21211.md), [National Basketball Association](22093.md), [Major League Baseball](38776.md), [Major League Soccer](71802.md), and the [National Hockey League](21809.md).[^cite_note-716][^cite_note-717][^cite_note-718][^cite_note-719] All these leagues, excluding soccer, are considered to be preeminent in their respective sports worldwide.&#x20;

While most major U.S. sports such as baseball and American football have evolved out of European practices, basketball, [volleyball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volleyball_in_the_United_States), [skateboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skateboarding), and [snowboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowboarding) are American inventions, many of which have become popular worldwide.[^cite_note-720] [Lacrosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacrosse_in_the_United_States) and [surfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surfing_in_the_United_States) arose from Native American and Native Hawaiian activities that predate European contact.[^cite_note-liss-721] The [U.S. professional sports market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_sports_market_in_the_United_States) was approximately \$69 billion in July 2013, roughly 50% larger than that of Europe, the Middle East, and Africa combined.[^cite_note-722]&#x20;

American football is by several measures the most popular spectator sport in the United States.[^cite_note-723] Though American football does not have a substantial following in other nations, the NFL has the highest average attendance (67,254)[^cite_note-724] of any professional sports league in the world, and generated over \$23 billion in revenue in the 2024 financial year.[^cite_note-Review-NFLRevenue2024-725] Baseball has been regarded as the U.S. "[national sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_sport)" since the late 19th century. The most-watched [individual sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_sport) in the U.S. are [golf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golf_in_the_United_States) and [auto racing](1022.md), particularly [NASCAR](21114.md) and [IndyCar](30446232.md).[^cite_note-726][^cite_note-727]&#x20;

On the [collegiate level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_athletics_in_the_United_States), the [NCAA](60706.md) reported \$1.1 billion in revenue for the 2017 fiscal year,[^cite_note-si-728] and [college football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_football) and [basketball](768856.md) attract large audiences, as the [NCAA March Madness tournament](507399.md) and the [College Football Playoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Football_Playoff) are some of the most watched national sporting events.[^cite_note-729] In the U.S., the intercollegiate sports level serves as the main feeder system for professional and Olympic sports, with significant exceptions such as [Minor League Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_League_Baseball). This differs greatly from practices in nearly all other countries, where publicly and privately funded sports organizations serve this function.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERosandich2002471-730]&#x20;

Eight [Olympic Games](22576.md) have taken place in the United States, beginning with the [1904 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1904_Summer_Olympics) in [St. Louis](27687.md), Missouri.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchausWenn2007224-731] The U.S. is scheduled to host the [2028 Summer Olympics](12695048.md) in [Los Angeles](18110.md) and the [2034 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2034_Winter_Olympics) in [Salt Lake City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Lake_City). [U.S. athletes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_at_the_Olympics) have won a total of 2,968 medals (1,179 gold) at the Olympic Games, the most of any country.[^cite_note-732][^cite_note-733][^cite_note-734]&#x20;

In other international competition, the United States is the home of a number of prestigious events, including the [America's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America%27s_Cup), [World Baseball Classic](1877416.md), the [U.S. Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Open_\(tennis\)), and the [Masters Tournament](157211.md). The [U.S. men's national soccer team](582488.md) has qualified for [eleven World Cups](40877989.md), while the [women's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_women%27s_national_soccer_team) has [won](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_at_the_FIFA_Women%27s_World_Cup) the [FIFA Women's World Cup](244862.md) and [Olympic soccer tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_at_the_Summer_Olympics) four and five times, respectively.[^cite_note-735] The [1999 FIFA Women's World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_FIFA_Women%27s_World_Cup) was hosted by the United States. [Its final match](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_FIFA_Women%27s_World_Cup_final) was attended by 90,185, setting the world record for largest women's sporting event crowd at the time.[^cite_note-736] The United States hosted the [1994 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_FIFA_World_Cup) and co-hosted, along with Canada and Mexico, the [2026 FIFA World Cup](36581929.md).[^cite_note-737]&#x20;

[^cite_note-5]: &#x20;D.C. stands for [District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia), the formal name of the U.S. [federal district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_district).

[^cite_note-officiallanguage-10]: &#x20;Per [Executive Order 14224](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_14224).<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-EOWP-6)</sup><sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-EONYT-7)</sup> States and territories variously recognize English only, English plus one or more local languages, or no language at all. See [§ Language](#Language). The [U.S. Congress](31756.md) has never passed legislation designating an official language at the federal level, and the [U.S. Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Constitution) does not specify an official language.<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-8)</sup><sup>[\[7\]](#cite_note-9)</sup>

[^cite_note-demonym-16]: &#x20;The historical and informal demonym [Yankee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yankee) has been applied to Americans, New Englanders, and northeasterners since the 18th century. Other terms, such as [Usonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usonia_\(name\)), are rare. For all lesser used terms, see [Demonyms for the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonyms_for_the_United_States).

[^cite_note-largestcountry-21]: &#x20;At 3,531,900 sq mi (9,147,590 km<sup>2</sup>), the United States is the third-largest country in the world by land area, behind [Russia](25391.md) and [China](5405.md). By total area (land and water), it is the third-largest, behind Russia and [Canada](5042916.md), if its coastal and territorial water areas are included. However, if only its internal waters are included (bays, sounds, rivers, lakes, and the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes)), the U.S. ranks fourth, after Russia, Canada, and China. \
    &#x20;Coastal/territorial waters included: 3,796,742 sq mi (9,833,517 km<sup>2</sup>)<sup>[\[15\]](#cite_note-CIA_Factbook_Area-19)</sup> \
    &#x20;Only internal waters included: 3,696,100 sq mi (9,572,900 km<sup>2</sup>)<sup>[\[16\]](#cite_note-BritannicaUS-20)</sup>

[^cite_note-pop-24]: &#x20; This figure excludes [Puerto Rico](23041.md) and the other [unincorporated islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unincorporated_territories_of_the_United_States) because they are counted separately in [U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._census) statistics.

[^cite_note-PPP-26]: &#x20;U.S. nominal and PPP-adjusted GDP are the same as the U.S. is the reference country for PPP calculations.

[^cite_note-28]: &#x20;After adjustment for taxes and transfers

[^cite_note-time-30]: &#x20; See [Time in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_in_the_United_States) for details about laws governing time zones in the United States.

[^cite_note-31]: &#x20;See [Date and time notation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_and_time_notation_in_the_United_States).

[^cite_note-34]: &#x20;The five major territories outside the union of states are [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa), [Guam](11974.md), the [Northern Mariana Islands](21402.md), [Puerto Rico](23041.md), and the [U.S. Virgin Islands](32135.md). The seven undisputed island areas without permanent populations are [Baker Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baker_Island), [Howland Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howland_Island), [Jarvis Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarvis_Island), [Johnston Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnston_Atoll), [Kingman Reef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingman_Reef), [Midway Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midway_Atoll), and [Palmyra Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_Atoll). U.S. sovereignty over the unpopulated [Bajo Nuevo Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajo_Nuevo_Bank), [Navassa Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navassa_Island), [Serranilla Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serranilla_Bank), and [Wake Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wake_Island) is disputed.<sup>[\[23\]](#cite_note-HRI-2012-33)</sup>

[^cite_note-39]: &#x20;The United States' world ranking by total area depends on how the country's area is measured. See [List of countries and dependencies by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) for information. The following two primary sources represent the range of estimates for the total areas of China and the United States: The *[Encyclopædia Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Britannica)* lists China as the third-largest country (after Russia and Canada), with a total area of 3,696,100 sq mi (9,572,900 km<sup>2</sup>).<sup>[\[24\]](#cite_note-35)</sup><sup>[\[25\]](#cite_note-36)</sup> The [CIA *World Factbook*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook) lists China as the fourth-largest country (after Russia, Canada and the United States), with a total area of 3,705,410 sq mi (9,596,960 km<sup>2</sup>).<sup>[\[15\]](#cite_note-CIA_Factbook_Area-19)</sup><sup>[\[26\]](#cite_note-37)</sup> Both sources exclude Taiwan as well as Chinese coastal and territorial waters from China's total. However, the CIA *World Factbook* includes U.S. coastal and territorial waters, while *Encyclopædia Britannica* excludes them. *Britannica* specifies the United States' area (excluding coastal and territorial waters) as 3,677,649 sq mi (9,525,067 km<sup>2</sup>), which is less than either source's figure for China.<sup>[\[27\]](#cite_note-38)</sup><sup>[\[16\]](#cite_note-BritannicaUS-20)</sup>

[^cite_note-pop_clock-40]: &#x20;The [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau)'s latest official population estimate of 341,784,857 residents (2025)<sup>[\[17\]](#cite_note-Vintage2025-22)</sup> is for the 50 states and the District of Columbia; it excludes the 3.6 million residents of the five major [U.S. territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States) and outlying islands. The Census Bureau also provides a continuously updated but unofficial population clock: [www.census.gov/popclock](https://www.census.gov/popclock/)

[^cite_note-46]: &#x20;The official [U.S. Government Publishing Office Style Manual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Government_Publishing_Office_Style_Manual) has prescribed specific usages for "U.S." and "United States" as part of official names. In "formal writing (treaties, Executive orders, proclamations, etc.); congressional bills; legal citations and courtwork; and covers and title pages",<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-gpo-stylemanual-45)</sup> "United States" is always used. In a sentence containing the name of another country, "United States" must be used. Otherwise, "U.S." is used preceding a government organization or as an adjective, but "United States" is used as an adjective preceding non-governmental organizations (e.g. [United States Steel Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Steel_Corporation)).<sup>[\[32\]](#cite_note-gpo-stylemanual-45)</sup>

[^cite_note-49]: &#x20;"Americus", which is derived from the Old High German first name "[Emmerich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmerich_\(name\))"

[^cite_note-51]: &#x20;*Americus* comes from the [Medieval Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin) name *Emericus* (for [Saint Emeric of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Emeric_of_Hungary)), itself derived from the Old High German name [Emmerich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmerich_\(name\)).

[^cite_note-84]: &#x20;From the late 15th century, the [Columbian exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_exchange) had been catastrophic for native populations throughout the Americas. It is estimated [that up to 95 percent of the Indigenous populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_soil_epidemic), especially in the Caribbean, [perished from infectious diseases during the years following European colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_disease_and_epidemics);<sup>[\[65\]](#cite_note-81)</sup> remaining populations were often displaced by European expansion.<sup>[\[66\]](#cite_note-82)</sup><sup>[\[67\]](#cite_note-83)</sup>

[^cite_note-126]: &#x20; [Meyer et al. 2001](#CITEREFMeyerSnowSnowCohen2001), From 1800 to 1900: "The discovery of gold in California in 1848 proved a momentous watershed for native people in the West. Hordes of single men stampeded to find fortune. Unrestrained by family, community, or church, they decimated the native population near the goldfields. California natives suffered the most complete genocide in U.S. history." Wolf, Jessica. ["Revealing the history of genocide against California's Native Americans"](https://newsroom.ucla.edu/stories/revealing-the-history-of-genocide-against-californias-native-americans). *UCLA Newsroom*. Retrieved July 8, 2018. [Madley 2016](#CITEREFMadley2016) [Smithers 2012](#CITEREFSmithers2012), p. 339: "The genocidal intent of California settlers and government officials was acted out in numerous battles and massacres (and aided by technological advances in weaponry, especially after the Civil War), in the abduction and sexual abuse of Indian women, and in the economic exploitation of Indian child labourers" [Blackhawk 2023](#CITEREFBlackhawk2023), p. 38: "With these works, a near consensus emerged. By most scholarly definitions and consistent with the UN Convention, these scholars all asserted that genocide against at least some Indigenous peoples had occurred in North America following colonisation, perpetuated first by colonial empires and then by independent nation-states"

"Prominent cross-national measures of democracy from the Varieties of Democracy Project (V-Dem), Bright Line Watch, and Freedom House, which had once ranked the country as a global leader, show a U.S. democracy slipping toward 'mixed regime' or 'illiberal democracy' status."

[^cite_note-:4-264]: &#x20;["DEMOCRACY REPORT 2026: Unraveling The Democratic Era?"](https://v-dem.net/documents/75/V-Dem_Institute_Democracy_Report_2026_lowres.pdf) (PDF). *[V-Dem Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute)*. March 17, 2026. Retrieved March 22, 2026. One notable shift is the transformation of the Republican Party to endorsing a far-right, nationalist, and anti-pluralist agenda. Nationalist, anti-liberal, far-right parties and leaders have largely driven the "third wave of autocratization." Yet the USA stands out as the only case where such movement seized control over one party in a rigid two-party system.

[^cite_note-:6-266]: &#x20;["Brian Klaas: "The United States is now a competitive authoritarian system""](https://blogs.lse.ac.uk/europpblog/2025/09/19/brian-klaas-is-the-united-states-still-a-democracy-competitive-authoritarian-system/). *EUROPP*. London School of Economics and Political Science. September 19, 2025. Retrieved September 8, 2026. Political scientists continue to debate this topic, but few would classify the United States as a robust democracy. It is either a democracy in crisis that is barely clinging onto the label, or one that has tipped over the edge into competitive authoritarianism - and I believe it's the latter.
